Azure Cloud-Native Platform Updates

Follow meaningful documentation updates across Microsoft's Azure cloud-native platform, spanning container orchestration and fleet management (AKS, AKS Automatic, Application Network, AKS Support, AKS Arc, Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes, and Kubernetes Fleet Manager), container registry (Azure Container Registry), application networking (Application Gateway for Containers), and Azure Linux. The tracker also summarizes the latest five AKS and Application Network releases.

It automatically scans for changes, then uses AI to summarize and highlight updates that are most likely to matter — such as new features, deprecations, and significant content revisions.

Minor edits (like typos, formatting tweaks, and other low-impact changes) are usually filtered out. Because the process is automated, some updates may be missed or summaries may not capture every nuance.

For complete accuracy, you can always follow the provided links to the original Microsoft documentation.

With this tracker, you can:

  • Quickly scan meaningful AKS, AKS Automatic, AKS Support, App Net, Arc K8s, AKS Arc, Azure Linux, ACR, AGC, and Fleet documentation changes from the past 7 days
  • Stay up to date with the latest AKS and App Net release notes without digging through every doc page
  • Search and browse CVE security data across AKS releases and VHD node images, powered by the AKS Vulnerability Data API (Public Preview)

AKS Releases

Latest 5 AKS and App Net release notes with AI-generated summaries, breaking changes, and Good to Know information.

updated: 27/11/2026, 00:17:07

Release 2026-10-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-27

The release on 2026-10-25 introduces automatic upgrades for unsupported Kubernetes versions and updates to node pools.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will automatically upgrade unsupported clusters after a grace period.
  • Default OS SKU for node pools changes to Ubuntu 26.04 with Kubernetes 1.38.
🔑

Key Features

  • Kubernetes 1.37 is now generally available as Long-Term Support.
  • Node Auto Provisioning (NAP) enhancements include new CRDs and features.
💡

Good to Know

  • Review upgrade channels to prepare for automated upgrades.
  • Community support for Kubernetes 1.34 ends by November 2026.

Release Notes 2026-09-25

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-10-01

The 2026-09-25 release introduces significant updates and deprecations. Key features include the general availability of identity bindings and preview support for Ubuntu 26.04.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • Windows Server 2022 is not supported in Kubernetes version 1.37 and above.
  • Flatcar Container Linux node images are removed, affecting scaling operations.
  • The annotation service.beta.kubernetes.io/azure-pip-tags no longer overrides public IP tags.
🔑

Key Features

  • Identity bindings are now generally available.
  • Ubuntu 26.04 is available in preview.
  • Windows Server OS SKU migration to 2025 is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • AKS will auto-migrate deprecated Availability Sets clusters after September 30, 2026.
  • AKS release order has been updated for Central US and East US.
  • Check AKS Release Tracker for detailed release timing.

Release 2026-09-04

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-09-09

This release introduces Kubernetes 1.37 and autoscaling for VM node pools. It also announces the retirement of Azure Linux with OS Guard.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AKS will remove Azure Linux with OS Guard node images on March 10, 2027.
  • AKS no longer produces VHDs for Windows Server 2019 and version 23H2.
🔑

Key Features

  • Autoscaling for Virtual Machines node pools is now generally available.
  • Kubernetes patch versions 1.36.3, 1.35.7, and 1.34.10 are available.
  • On-Demand Monitor for node health checks is in preview.
💡

Good to Know

  • LocalDNS is enabled automatically for compatible cluster networking.
  • AKS rejects updates that remove IPv6 from dual-stack clusters.
  • Managed namespace creation rejects names starting with reserved prefixes.

AppNet 2026-08-19

App Net
View Release
📅 2026-08-31

AppNet is now in public preview with several updates and bug fixes.

❌

Breaking Changes

  • AppNet 1.0, 1.1, and 1.2 versions retired.
  • Moving AppNet resources across resource groups is now blocked.
🔑

Key Features

  • AppNet available in more Azure regions.
  • AppNet 1.5 updated to Istio 1.30.3.
💡

Good to Know

  • Cluster prerequisites validated at member-join time.
  • Fixed issues with member updates and version upgrades.

Release 2026-08-07

AKS
View Release
📅 2026-08-11

This release introduces several enhancements including Node Auto Provisioning and automatic availability zone placement.

🔑

Key Features

  • Node Auto Provisioning now supports restricted public network access
  • Automatic availability zone placement is enabled globally
  • Control-plane only upgrades to AKS Long Term Support are allowed
  • Node pool rollback feature is now generally available
  • Encryption in Transit for Azure File CSI driver is now available

AKS and Azure Product Retirements

Upcoming retirements and retirements from the past 12 months, plus notices without a confirmed date. Dates and recommended actions come from Microsoft Azure Updates.

21 notices

Retirement: Managed NGINX Ingress with Application Routing Add-on Retiring November 2026

AKS
Due within 30 days
Retirement: November 30, 2026 Notice updated: 2026-02-27
The upstream Ingress-NGINX project is being deprecated and will stop receiving updates after March 2026. Microsoft will continue providing critical security patches through 30 November 2026, after which the NGINX ingress controller will no longer be supported within the Application Routing add-on. Supported alternatives include Gateway API with Application Routing (in development), Application Gateway for Containers, and Istio Service Mesh. Learn more.

Retirement: Azure Linux with OS Guard in Azure Kubernetes Service

AKS Azure Linux
Due within 30 days
Retirement: December 10, 2026 Notice updated: 2026-09-11
Support for Azure Linux with OS Guard in Azure Kubernetes Service will end on December 10, 2026. Azure Container Linux is the replacement for Azure Linux with OS Guard (preview). Starting on December 10, 2026: - Users won’t be able to create new Azure Linux with OS Guard (preview) node pools. - AKS won’t produce new node images for affected node pools. - Existing node pools won’t receive security patches. Read moreRead less
Support for Azure Linux with OS Guard in Azure Kubernetes Service will end on December 10, 2026. Azure Container Linux is the replacement for Azure Linux with OS Guard (preview). Starting on December 10, 2026: - Users won’t be able to create new Azure Linux with OS Guard (preview) node pools. - AKS won’t produce new node images for affected node pools. - Existing node pools won’t receive security patches. On March 10, 2027, AKS will remove the Azure Linux with OS Guard node images. After the images are removed, scaling and remediation operations, including reimage and redeploy operations, will fail for affected node pools. You can use either of the following migration options: - Migrate an existing Azure Linux with OS Guard node pool in place by following the Migrate nodes to Azure Container Linux for Azure Kubernetes Service documentation. This operation preforms a standard node image upgrade. - Alternatively, create a new Azure Container Linux node pool, migrate and validate workloads, and then delete the Azure Linux with OS Guard node pool. Follow Add Azure Container Linux node pools and remove existing node pools. For other available options, see the AKS node operating system. If you have questions, get answers from community experts in Microsoft Q&A or AKS GitHub Repository. If you have a support plan and need technical help, create a support request.
View Microsoft notice

On 15 March 2027, Windows Server 2022 will be retired when Kubernetes 1.34 reaches the end of platform support. You will not be able to create new Windows Server 2022 node pools on Kubernetes 1.35 and above. We encourage you to make the switch before 15 March 2027 to gain the richer benefits of Windows Server 2025 or Windows Server Annual Channel. These new Windows OS versions will be supported on AKS before Windows Server 2022 is retired. For more updates, see our AKS public roadmap. Required action From now through 14 March 2027, you can continue to use Windows Server 2022 on Kubernetes version 1.34 and below. After 15 March 2027, Windows Server 2022 won't be supported. You won’t be able to create Windows Server 2022 node pools on Kubernetes 1.35 and above. Follow the detailed steps in our documentation to upgrade to a supported Windows Server version (Windows Server 2025 or Windows Server Annual Channel) by 15 March 2027. Help and Support If you have questions, get answers from community experts in Microsoft Q&A. If you have a support plan and you need technical help, please create a support request.

Read moreRead less
Ubuntu 20.04 on Azure Kubernetes Service will be retired on March 17, 2027. Please transition to a supported Ubuntu version by that date. Ubuntu 20.04 has been replaced by newer supported versions, including Ubuntu 22.04 and Ubuntu 24.04. We encourage you to transition to Ubuntu 22.04+ before the retirement date. Starting on March 17, 2027, AKS will no longer create new node images for Ubuntu 20.04 or provide security updates. Existing node images will be deleted. Your node pools will be unsupported and you will no longer be able to scale. Required action: If you’re currently using Ubuntu 20.04 with a Confidential VM (CVM) on AKS, follow our instructions to upgrade your Kubernetes version to 1.33+ where Ubuntu 24.04 will be the default Ubuntu version for CVM workloads. If you’re currently using Ubuntu 20.04 with Federal Information Process Standard (FIPS) on AKS, follow our instructions to upgrade your Kubernetes version to 1.34+ where Ubuntu 22.04 will be the default Ubuntu version for FIPS workloads. To avoid service disruptions, scaling restrictions, and remain supported, please follow our instructions to upgrade your Kubernetes version to 1.34+ by March 17, 2027. For more information on this retirement, see AKS GitHub. More Information: If you have questions, get answers from community experts in Microsoft Q&A. If you have a support plan and you need technical help, create a support request. Learn more.

Read moreRead less
On March 31, 2027 Azure Monitor will be deprecating the sidecar for remote-write of Prometheus metrics to Azure Monitor Workspace. This is as part of our ongoing efforts to improve reliability and reduce complexity. We recommend configuring your self-hosted Prometheus or Prometheus Operator to remote-write directly to Azure Monitor Workspace. All authentication methods are supported in the Prometheus remote-write configuration, and direct remote-write is fully compatible with the Prometheus Operator. You can find detailed configuration guidance in the official documentation. This approach removes the need for the sidecar and simplifies your setup. We encourage you to transition to direct Prometheus remote-write as soon as possible to take advantage of the improved experience and ensure uninterrupted service. If you have any questions or need assistance during this transition, please feel free to contact ciprometheus@microsoft.com with your name, company name, email contact and subscription IDs.

Read moreRead less
On June 30, 2027, we'll retire Ubuntu 22.04 on Azure Kubernetes Service. To avoid disruptions, transition to Ubuntu 24.04 or later by that date. Newer supported versions include kernel updates and security improvements. Until June 30, 2027, you can continue to use Ubuntu 22.04 on AKS without disruption. If you don't migrate by June 30, 2027, you won't be able to create new node pools, AKS won't produce new node images, and you'll no longer receive security patches for existing node pools. If you'd like to enable long-term support with Kubernetes version 1.33+, first update your node pools to Ubuntu 24.04. On April 30, 2028 AKS will remove Ubuntu 22.04 node images and existing code, causing scaling and remediation operations to fail. Migration Options: - Default Operating System SKU (OSSku): If you're using 'Ubuntu,' you'll automatically migrate to Ubuntu 24.04 when you upgrade your Kubernetes version to 1.35 or later. - Versioned OSSku: If you're using 'Ubuntu2204,' update your OSSku to 'Ubuntu' for Kubernetes 1.35 or later, or 'Ubuntu2404' for Kubernetes 1.32 or later. Learn more: - Get answers from community experts in Microsoft Q&A or AKS GitHub. - For support plan and need technical help, follow the documentation - Find out about service retirements that may impact your resources in the Azure Retirement Workbook. Retirements may not be visible in the workbook for up to two weeks after being announced.

Following the archival of the Open Service Mesh project, the Istio service mesh add-on was released for Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) and is Generally Available since February 28,2024. Due to the upstream archival of open-source Open Service Mesh, the Open Service Mesh add-on for AKS will no longer receive any new minor version releases or new features and will be retired on September 30, 2027. Security patch updates will be provided up until September 30, 2027. You can continue to use the existing Open Service Mesh add-on for Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) until September 30, 2027 or transition to Istio add-on for Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) before that time. Migration guidance from Open Service Mesh (OSM) configurations to Istio can be found here. There are no changes in pricing between the two add-ons.

Retirement: Pod name dimension in AKS pod platform metrics

AKS
Upcoming retirement
Retirement: September 30, 2027 Notice updated: 2026-10-06
Beginning September 30, 2027, support for the pod name dimension on the following Azure Monitor platform metrics is retiring, and these metrics are transitioning to aggregate pod counters: - Number of pods by phase (kube_pod_status_phase) - Number of pods in Ready state (kube_pod_status_ready) After this change: - Metric names remain unchanged. - Aggregate pod counts remain available. - Namespace-level monitoring scenarios remain supported. - The pod name dimension will no longer be available for filtering, grouping, or alerting. During the deprecation period, AKS will maintain both the...
Read moreRead less
Beginning September 30, 2027, support for the pod name dimension on the following Azure Monitor platform metrics is retiring, and these metrics are transitioning to aggregate pod counters: - Number of pods by phase (kube_pod_status_phase) - Number of pods in Ready state (kube_pod_status_ready) After this change: - Metric names remain unchanged. - Aggregate pod counts remain available. - Namespace-level monitoring scenarios remain supported. - The pod name dimension will no longer be available for filtering, grouping, or alerting. During the deprecation period, AKS will maintain both the existing and the new aggregate implementations. Both are populated with data, existing monitoring experiences will continue to function, and you will have at least one year of historical data in the aggregate implementation before retirement. You may be affected if any dashboard, workbook, alert, automation, or monitoring workflow: - Uses kube_pod_status_phase or kube_pod_status_ready and filters on the pod name dimension. - Splits or groups results by the pod name dimension. Recommended action: - If you use aggregate pod counts, no action is required. - If you use the pod name dimension, update dashboards, alerts, workbooks, and automation before September 30, 2027. For pod-level monitoring and troubleshooting, we recommend using Azure Monitor Managed Prometheus and Kubernetes-native pod metrics. Help and support If you have questions, get answers from community experts in Microsoft Q&A.
View Microsoft notice

Retirement: Azure Key Vault Secrets Provider Extension for Azure Arc enabled Kubernetes clusters

Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes
Upcoming retirement
Retirement: October 9, 2027 Notice updated: 2026-10-09
The Azure Key Vault Secrets Provider Extension for Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes will be retired on October 9, 2027. Customers currently using the extension should plan to migrate to the Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension before the retirement date. The Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension provides equivalent functionality for accessing and synchronizing secrets from Azure Key Vault for workloads running on Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes clusters. In addition, the Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension includes enhancements such as support for offline access to secrets and authentication...
Read moreRead less
The Azure Key Vault Secrets Provider Extension for Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes will be retired on October 9, 2027. Customers currently using the extension should plan to migrate to the Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension before the retirement date. The Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension provides equivalent functionality for accessing and synchronizing secrets from Azure Key Vault for workloads running on Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes clusters. In addition, the Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension includes enhancements such as support for offline access to secrets and authentication using workload identity federation, enabling applications to access Azure Key Vault without storing long-lived credentials on the cluster. After retirement, the Azure Key Vault Secrets Provider Extension will no longer receive new features, bug fixes, security updates, or technical support. Migration guidance is available to help customers transition their workloads and configurations to the Azure Key Vault Secret Store Extension ahead of retirement.
View Microsoft notice

Retirement: Docker Content Trust on March 31, 2028 – Replaced by the Notary Project and Azure Key Vault

Azure Container Registry
Upcoming retirement
Retirement: March 31, 2028 Notice updated: 2025-03-26
Azure Container Registry (ACR) implements Docker Content Trust (DCT), allowing image publishers to sign their images and image consumers to verify that the images they pull are signed. With advancements in technology, Docker Content Trust no longer meets the requirements of modern supply chain security for containers. As a result, Docker Content Trust will be deprecated and no longer available in ACR after March 31, 2028. If you currently use Docker Content Trust, you should transition to the Notary Project ecosystem, including their open-source supply chain tool, Notation. How does this...
Read moreRead less
Azure Container Registry (ACR) implements Docker Content Trust (DCT), allowing image publishers to sign their images and image consumers to verify that the images they pull are signed. With advancements in technology, Docker Content Trust no longer meets the requirements of modern supply chain security for containers. As a result, Docker Content Trust will be deprecated and no longer available in ACR after March 31, 2028. If you currently use Docker Content Trust, you should transition to the Notary Project ecosystem, including their open-source supply chain tool, Notation. How does this affect me? - Starting September 30, 2025, customers will no longer be able to enable Docker Content Trust on Azure Container Registry. - Data stored in Azure Container Registry for signing and verifying signatures with Docker Content Trust will be permanently deleted on March 31, 2028. - Docker Content Trust function will be completely removed from Azure Container Registry on March 31, 2028. Required Action Start your transition to Notary Project today and enhance the security and trust of your container ecosystem. Use the following articles to get started. Signing: - Use AKV with self-signed certificates - Use AKV with CA-issued certificates - Sign in ADO pipelines - Sign in GitHub workflows Verification: - Verify in ADO pipelines - Verify in GitHub workflows - Verify on AKS
View Microsoft notice

Retirement: Kubenet Networking for Azure Kubernetes Service to Be Retired on March 31, 2028

AKS
Upcoming retirement
Retirement: March 31, 2028 Notice updated: 2025-03-18
On March 31, 2028, kubenet networking for Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) will be retired. To avoid service disruptions, you'll need to follow these instructions to upgrade to Azure Container Networking Interface (CNI) overlay before that date, when workloads running on kubenet for AKS will no longer be supported. Azure CNI overlay provides the same IP address overlay architecture as kubenet, plus new capabilities for increased scalability. We encourage you to migrate to Azure CNI overlay prior to the retirement date to take advantage of the added benefits.

On September 30, 2028, we’ll end support for Azure Network Policy Manager (NPM) on Linux nodes in AKS. To avoid disruptions, migrate AKS clusters running Linux nodes from NPM to Cilium Network Policy by that date. Transitioning to Cilium Network Policy for AKS clusters using Azure CNI powered by Cilium provides significant improvements, including: - Robust support for Kubernetes-native policies. - Powerful extended features such as Layer 7 policy, and Fully Qualified Domain Name filtering. - eBPF-based architecture for packet processing, offering better performance, scalability and security. Windows Server Annual Channel (Preview) on AKS will be retired on May 15, 2026, please transition to Long Term Servicing Channel (LTSC) by that date. We encourage you to transition to Long Term Servicing Channel (LTSC) prior to the retirement date to experience the benefits of LTSC including longer support cycles and increased stability. We encourage you to transition to Azure Kubernetes Service on Azure Local before March 16, 2026, to benefit from its enhanced capabilities. More information If you have additional questions, please contact us through the AKS enabled by Azure Arc GitHub repository. Learn more about service retirements that may impact your resources in the Azure Retirement Workbook. Please note that retirements may not be visible in the workbook for up to two weeks after being announced.

Read moreRead less
Azure Linux 2.0 on AKS will be retired on November 30, 2025, please transition to Azure Linux 3.0 by that date. Azure Linux 2.0 has been replaced by the newer supported version Azure Linux 3.0. We encourage you to transition to Azure Linux 3.0 prior to the retirement date to experience the capabilities of Azure Linux 3.0 including kernel updates and security improvements. From now to November 30, 2025, you can continue to use Azure Linux 2.0 without disruption. On November 30, 2025, Azure Linux 2.0 will no longer be supported on AKS; AKS will no longer create new node pools, produce new node images, or provide security updates for Azure Linux 2.0 after that date. On March 31, 2026, Azure Linux 2.0 node images will be removed. This will cause scaling operations to fail. Customers can transition to Azure Linux 3.0 by taking one of the following actions: - If you’re currently using Azure Linux 2.0 on AKS, please follow our instructions to upgrade your Kubernetes version to 1.32+. - Alternately, starting Kubernetes version 1.28+ you can perform an in-place node pool update from --os-sku AzureLinux to --os-sku AzureLinux3, enabling you to migrate existing node pools from Azure Linux 2.0 to 3.0 while remaining on the same k8s version. Your node pool will automatically reimage to the equivalent Azure Linux 3.0 node image. See documentation here. To avoid service disruptions, please follow our instructions to migrate to Azure Linux 3.0 by November 30, 2025.

On Azure Linux, the */etc/ssl/certs* path is a symbolic link to */etc/pki/tls/certs*. If a container expects to map */etc/ssl/certs* to use the *ca-certificates.crt* certificate file on Azure Linux, the container instead gets a symbolic link that points to nowhere. This behavior causes certificate-related errors in the container. The container also has to map */etc/pki* so that the container can follow the symbolic link chain. If the container has to work on both Ubuntu and Azure Linux hosts, you can map */etc/pki* by using the `DirectoryOrCreate` type in a [hostPath volume](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/storage/volumes/#hostpath).  ### Step 4: Update Azure CLI and the AKS preview extension -If you try to deploy an Azure Linux AKS cluster by using Azure CLI, you might receive an error message that states that the `AzureLinux` option isn't supported for the `OSSku` parameter. This message means that you might be using an outdated version of the Azure CLI or the AKS preview extension.                                                                                       @@ -8,10 +8,25 @@ URL redirects take advantage of the [RequestRedirect rule filter](https://gatewa  ## Redirection -A redirect sets the response status code returned to clients to understand the purpose of the redirect. The following types of redirection are supported:+A redirect sets the response status code that Application Gateway for Containers returns to clients. This status code helps clients understand the purpose of the redirect. Application Gateway for Containers supports the following status codes in the `statusCode` field of the RequestRedirect filter: -- 301 (Moved permanently): Indicates that the target resource is assigned a new permanent URI. Any future references to this resource use one of the enclosed URIs. Use 301 status code for HTTP to HTTPS redirection.-- 302 (Found): Indicates that the target resource is temporarily under a different URI. Since the redirection can change on occasion, the client should continue to use the effective request URI for future requests.+| Status code | Meaning | Permanent | Preserves method and body |+| --- | --- | --- | --- |+| 301 | Moved Permanently | Yes | No. Clients may change POST to GET. |+| 302 | Found | No | No. Clients may change POST to GET. |+| 303 | See Other | No | No. The client follows up with GET (or HEAD) and drops the body. |+| 307 | Temporary Redirect | No | Yes. A client that automatically follows the redirect must preserve the method and body. |+| 308 | Permanent Redirect | Yes | Yes. A client that automatically follows the redirect must preserve the method and body. |++- **301 (Moved Permanently):** The target resource has a new permanent URI. Future references to this resource should use one of the enclosed URIs. Use 301 for HTTP to HTTPS redirection of browser traffic.+- **302 (Found):** The target resource is temporarily under a different URI. Since the redirection can change, the client should continue to use the effective request URI for future requests.+- **303 (See Other):** The target resource redirects the user agent to a different resource, as indicated by a URI in the `Location` header field.+- **307 (Temporary Redirect):** The resource is temporarily available at another URI. A client that automatically follows the redirect must preserve the request method and body. Future requests should continue using the original URI.+- **308 (Permanent Redirect):** The resource has a new permanent URI. A client that automatically follows the redirect must preserve the request method and body. Future requests should use the new URI.++Tip++Many clients rewrite a POST to a GET when they follow a 301 or 302 redirect, and the request body is dropped. If the redirected request isn't a GET or HEAD, use 307 or 308 instead. For example, use 308 rather than 301 for a permanent HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect when POST or other method/body-sensitive requests must retain their semantics.  ## Redirection capabilities

Check if any scheduling constraints are restricting pod placement across available nodes. +## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Insufficient memory++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y Insufficient memory.++### Cause++The pod's effective memory request exceeds the remaining allocatable request budget on eligible nodes.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the insufficient memory issue:++1. This resource is separate from a PVC or Azure Disk's requested size.++This scheduler rejection differs from a pod that was scheduled and later evicted because of local-storage pressure. Memory-backed `emptyDir` volumes consume memory rather than this local-storage budget.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the insufficient ephemeral-storage issue:++1. Inspect requests, node allocatable storage, and allocated resources:++    ```bash+    kubectl get pod <pod-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl describe node <eligible-node>+    ```+2. Correct erroneous units or an unrealistic request. Size the request for the workload's writable layer, disk-backed `emptyDir`, logs, and other applicable local storage.+3. If the request is legitimate, provide suitable eligible node storage or capacity. Increasing a PVC's size doesn't increase node ephemeral storage.+4. If events instead show eviction or `DiskPressure`, investigate consumption and the node condition rather than treating it as this request-fit error.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y Insufficient nvidia.com/gpu.++### Cause++Eligible nodes can't satisfy the pod's advertised `nvidia.com/gpu` request. Possible conditions include:++- No suitable GPU node is available.+- GPU requests from other pods reserve the exposed devices.+- A GPU VM exists, but the supported driver or device-plugin configuration isn't healthy or doesn't expose the expected resource.+- Taints, selectors, or affinity exclude otherwise usable GPU nodes.++A mixed rejection message doesn't establish which of these conditions caused a specific report.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the insufficient GPU issue:++1. Inspect the live GPU request and node resource advertisement:++    ```bash+    kubectl get pod <pod-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl describe node <gpu-node>+    kubectl get daemonsets,pods -n kube-system -o wide+    ```+2. Distinguish absent allocatable GPU resources from resources already reserved by requests. A GPU VM size or low observed GPU utilization alone isn't sufficient.+3. Verify the GPU pool's supported configuration and driver/device-plugin health. Follow [managed AKS GPU guidance](/en-us/azure/aks/aks-managed-gpu-nodes); don't install a second device plugin indiscriminately into a managed configuration.+4. For genuine capacity shortage, make an eligible GPU node available and inspect scale-up events, SKU restrictions, quota, and allocation failures. The [official AKS/KEDA example](/en-us/azure/aks/autoscale-gpu-workloads-with-keda) documents this scheduling error as a capacity signal.+5. Correct compatible placement/tolerations where necessary. Don't send an application to system nodes merely to bypass `CriticalAddonsOnly`.+6. Verify both scheduling and GPU functionality. Removing the GPU request isn't a validated remedy for a GPU-dependent application.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Node selector, affinity, or anti-affinity rules++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with one of the following scheduler errors.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't match Pod's node affinity or selector.+>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't match pod affinity rules.+>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't match pod anti-affinity rules.+>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't satisfy existing pods anti-affinity rules.++### Cause++A required placement rule excludes the candidate nodes. The rule can apply to node labels or to the location of other pods:++- **Node selector/node affinity:** Node labels don't satisfy the pod's `nodeSelector` or required node affinity. If you configure both, the node labels must satisfy both. All selector labels must match. Required affinity terms are alternatives, while expressions within a term must all match.+- **Pod affinity:** No matching pod is found in the required namespace and topology domain.+- **Pod anti-affinity:** The incoming pod excludes domains occupied by matching pods, or an existing pod's rule excludes the incoming pod from its domain. An existing pod's rule can reject the incoming pod even if the incoming pod has no anti-affinity.++Preferred affinity rules alone don't cause these hard rejections. For self-affinity, a pod that matches its own selector can schedule without an peer when the other first-pod exception conditions are met.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the placement rule mismatch:++1. Inspect the live pod's selectors and affinity rules, and compare them with node labels:++    ```bash+    kubectl get pod <pod-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl get nodes --show-labels+    ```+2. For pod affinity or anti-affinity, inspect pods in the relevant namespaces and topology domains. Check label selectors, namespace scope, and `topologyKey`. For anti-affinity, also inspect existing pods' rules:++    ```bash+    kubectl get pods -A -o wide+    kubectl get pod <existing-pod-name> -n <existing-pod-namespace> -o yaml+    ```+3. Apply the fix for the affected rule:++    **Node selector/node affinity:** Correct mistaken or stale selector or label values, or provide nodes that meet the requirement.++    **Pod affinity:** Restore the intended matching pod or correct the selector, namespace, or topology key. The matching pod must be in a domain the incoming pod can also use.++    **Pod anti-affinity:** Make another genuine topology domain eligible by adding suitable nodes or correcting an overly restrictive node selector. Keep all applicable required anti-affinity exclusions satisfied.+4. Make workload changes through the controller's pod template. Correct unintended rules or label matches, and relax required rules only when hardware, security, and availability requirements permit it. Don't falsify hardware or topology labels, delete unrelated production peers as a routine fix, or use `spec.nodeName` to bypass scheduling.+5. Confirm the pending or replacement pod schedules on an eligible node in a permitted topology domain. Recheck current events and verify that both incoming and existing pods' required policies are satisfied.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't match pod topology spread constraints++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't match pod topology spread constraints.++### Cause++A hard spread constraint (`whenUnsatisfiable: DoNotSchedule`) can't place the pod without exceeding `maxSkew`. Eligible domains depend on topology labels, selectors or affinity, and the configured node inclusion policies.++When there are fewer eligible domains than `minDomains`, the global minimum becomes zero. A cluster can have several nodes but only one eligible domain for a particular pod.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the topology spread constraint issue:++1. Inspect the constraint, selected replicas, and node labels:++    ```bash+    kubectl get pod <pod-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl get pods -n <namespace> -l <spread-selector> -o wide+    kubectl get nodes --show-labels+    ```++    - Check `topologyKey`, `labelSelector`, `maxSkew`, `minDomains`, `nodeAffinityPolicy`, and `nodeTaintsPolicy`. Restore sufficient genuinely eligible domains or correct an unintended restriction.+2. Change skew or domain requirements only after assessing availability intent. `ScheduleAnyway` makes the policy soft; successful placement after that change doesn't prove the original hard requirement is met.+3. Verify the resulting distribution.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y Too many pods++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y Too many pods.++### Cause++Eligible nodes reached the scheduler's pod-count limit. Available CPU or memory doesn't override node allocatable `pods` or node pool `maxPods`.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the pod-count limit issue:++1. Check the limit and assigned pods.++    ```bash+    kubectl describe node <eligible-node>+    kubectl get pods -A --field-selector spec.nodeName=<eligible-node> -o wide+    ```+2. Account for system DaemonSets and other workloads, not only the application's replicas.+3. Release capacity by removing only legitimately obsolete workloads, or add eligible nodes. If changing `maxPods` requires a new pool, plan that change with the supported AKS networking/IP limits.+4. Confirm the pending pod schedules after a slot becomes available. Don't patch node capacity to fabricate slots.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes were unschedulable++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes were unschedulable.++### Cause++All eligible nodes are cordoned (`spec.unschedulable: true`) or otherwise excluded by the corresponding scheduling condition. This condition can be intentional during maintenance.++A condition-related untolerated-taint message can also mention `node.kubernetes.io/unschedulable`.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the unschedulable node issue:++1. Inspect the node and maintenance context.++    ```bash+    kubectl get node <node-name> -o yaml+    kubectl describe pod <pod-name> -n <namespace>+    ```+2. If maintenance is complete and the node is safe to use, uncordon that specific node.++    ```bash+    kubectl uncordon <node-name>+    ```+3. Otherwise, provide another eligible node or wait for authorized maintenance to complete. Don't globally uncordon nodes or add a blanket toleration to bypass maintenance.+4. Confirm node state and pod scheduling.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: pod has unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims.++### Cause++A claim that must bind before scheduling is still unbound. Possible causes include an incorrect or unavailable provisioner, provisioning or authorization failures, missing storage capacity, incompatible volume matching, or storage topology.++A pending `WaitForFirstConsumer` claim alone isn't this error.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaim issue:++1. Inspect the claim's events and class.++    ```bash+    kubectl describe pvc <pvc-name> -n <namespace>+    kubectl get pvc <pvc-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl get storageclass <storageclass-name> -o yaml+    ```+2. Verify the provisioner is available and inspect its controllers and events. Resolve the actual authorization, SKU, capacity, or binding error rather than scaling CPU nodes blindly.+3. For new topology-aware Azure Disk claims, choose an appropriate working class by using `WaitForFirstConsumer`.+4. Preserve existing data and reclaim-policy semantics. Before replacing an unbound claim, verify that it has no data to preserve and that replacement is appropriate. Don't delete production claims as a generic binding remedy.+5. Confirm binding, pod scheduling, successful mounting, and a real data-access check.++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes exceed max volume count++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes exceed max volume count.++### Cause++The pod's distinct CSI volumes, together with volumes already needed on the node, exceed the driver's advertised per-node attachment count. This condition isn't a disk-size limit or a CPU shortage.++### Solution++Follow these steps to resolve the maximum volume count issue:++1. Inspect the real driver limit and claims:++    ```bash+    kubectl get csinode <eligible-node> -o yaml+    kubectl get pod <pod-name> -n <namespace> -o yaml+    kubectl get pvc -n <namespace>+    ```+2. For Azure Disks, examine `disk.csi.azure.com` under `CSINode.spec.drivers`, including `allocatable.count`. Compare volume demand from this and other assigned pods.+3. Place the workload on an eligible node size with sufficient real attachment capacity, distribute workloads appropriately, or use a storage design suitable for the required volume count.+4. Keep storage topology and access modes compatible. Fewer GiB per disk don't reduce the number of attachments.+5. Confirm every required volume mounts and supports real data access. Don't patch CSI limits or fabricate volume handles. See [Azure Disk per-node volume guidance](/en-us/azure/aks/azure-disks-dynamic-pv).++## Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't have free ports for the requested pod ports++Pods remain in the **Pending** state with the following scheduler error.++>+> Error: 0/(X) nodes are available: Y nodes didn't have free ports for the requested pod ports.++### Cause++Another assigned pod reserves an overlapping `hostPort` on eligible nodes. If the workload doesn't need direct host-port binding, use an appropriate Service design instead.+4. Confirm scheduling and the application's intended network behavior. Successful scheduling alone doesn't confirm that an application is listening.+ ## References  - [Kubernetes: Use Azure Disks with Azure Kubernetes Service](/en-us/azure/aks/azure-disks-dynamic-pv) - [Kubernetes: Use node taints](/en-us/azure/aks/use-node-taints) - [Kubernetes Documentation: Insufficient CPU](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/resource-bin-packing/#insufficient-resource)-- [Kubernetes Documentation: Assign and Schedule Pods with Taints and Tolerations](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/taint-and-toleration/)+- [Managed AKS GPU node guidance](/en-us/azure/aks/aks-managed-gpu-nodes)+- [AKS GPU autoscaling example](/en-us/azure/aks/autoscale-gpu-workloads-with-keda)  **Third-party contact disclaimer**

This is commonly observed in redirecting an old domain name to a new domain name; such as `contoso.com` to `fabrikam.com`.-- Path redirection has two different variants: `prefix` and `full`.+- Use protocol redirection to move the client from an unencrypted traffic scheme to an encrypted one, such as HTTP to HTTPS redirection.+- Use hostname redirection to match the fully qualified domain name (FQDN) of the request. Commonly, you use this type of redirection to redirect an old domain name to a new domain name, such as `contoso.com` to `fabrikam.com`.+- Use path redirection to replace parts of the path. This type of redirection has two variants: `ReplacePrefixMatch` and `ReplaceFullPath`. -    - `Prefix` redirection type will redirect all requests starting with a defined value. For example: a prefix of /shop would match /shop and any text after. For example, /shop, /shop/checkout, and /shop/item-a would all redirect to /shop as well.-    - `Full` redirection type matches an exact value. For example: /shop could redirect to /store, but /shop/checkout wouldn't redirect to /store.+    - `ReplacePrefixMatch` replaces only the matched prefix and keeps the rest of the path. For example, with a `PathPrefix` match of `/shop` and a replacement of `/store`, `/shop` redirects to `/store` and `/shop/checkout` redirects to `/store/checkout`.+    - `ReplaceFullPath` replaces the entire path with a fixed value. It can be enabled on clusters that are automatically upgraded (such as [AKS Automatic](intro-aks-automatic) clusters), but it isn't upgraded when the cluster auto-upgrades, so an out-of-date mesh revision can eventually block the cluster's automatic upgrade. [Upgrade the add-on](istio-upgrade) to keep it compatible with the cluster's Kubernetes version.
